# Science source ledger

Every physical number shown to students, where it lives, and the source it was checked against.
Checked 16 October 2026 by opening each source (journal or arXiv page, ar5iv full text,
Crossref, JPL SSD/Horizons, NAIF, PDS, NSSDC, SIMBAD, VizieR). Where a publisher blocked access,
the arXiv copy or the archived data were read instead; the note says so. The Orrery and Deep Field
rows were re-checked on 19 October 2026 after a science review of both simulations.

`cs.json` files mirror `en.json` line for line, so each `en.json` row covers both catalogs.

| Status          | Meaning                                                                             |
| --------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| verified        | Matches the opened source at the precision shown.                                   |
| derived         | Computed from verified inputs; the note gives the arithmetic.                       |
| teaching-choice | A deliberate model or display value, labelled as such in the app or code.           |
| **flag**        | Not confirmed in an opened source; kept and listed under [Open flags](#open-flags). |

Unit constants used for derivations: GM☉ = 1.32712440041 × 10²⁰ m³ s⁻² (DE430, TDB; the IAU 2015 B3
nominal (GM)☉ = 1.3271244 × 10²⁰ m³ s⁻² is what the code uses), so
GM☉/c² = 1.4766 km and GM☉/c³ = 4.9255 μs; 1 kpc = 3,261.56 ly; 1 au = 149,597,870.7 km (IAU 2012 B2).

## Card catalogues

Every figure a card prints comes from a catalogue: the solar system's (`solar/catalogue.ts`,
shown by both the Orrery and Deep Field), the Orrery's timeline (`orrery/catalogue/`), the
Galaxy's and the nearest stars' (`galaxy/catalogue/`), the cosmic map's (`cosmos/catalogue.ts`)
and the probes' (`solar/probes.ts`), each with its unit, range, status and a source id
from the table below. Cards format it in the visitor's distance unit
and language; the rows further down audit how the model values were chosen.

| Engine                            | Process                     | Source                           | Status   | Note                                                                                                                                        |
| --------------------------------- | --------------------------- | -------------------------------- | -------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `stellar/engines.psr-j0030`       | spinDown                    | `riley2019`                      | derived  | a rotation-powered millisecond pulsar: no fusion; its slowing spin powers its beams and heats its polar caps                                |
| `stellar/engines.sirius-b`        | cooling                     | `kippenhahn2012`, `bond2017`     | derived  | a white dwarf: no fusion; it shines by the heat it stored and slowly cools                                                                  |
| `stellar/engines.lhs-6343-c`      | contraction                 | `chabrier2000`                   | derived  | a brown dwarf below the hydrogen-burning limit: it burned its deuterium young and now glows by slow contraction and cooling                 |
| `stellar/engines.trappist-1`      | ppChain                     | `chabrier2000`                   | derived  | a red dwarf just above the hydrogen-burning limit, fully convective, so all its hydrogen passes through the core                            |
| `stellar/engines.proxima`         | ppChain                     | `chabrier2000`                   | derived  | a fully convective red dwarf: all its hydrogen passes through the core                                                                      |
| `stellar/engines.alpha-cen-a`     | ppChain                     | `kippenhahn2012`, `akeson2021`   | derived  | a main-sequence star a little heavier than the Sun, burning hydrogen in its core; from its mass and stage by standard stellar models        |
| `stellar/engines.alpha-cen-b`     | ppChain                     | `kippenhahn2012`, `akeson2021`   | derived  | a main-sequence star a little lighter than the Sun, burning hydrogen in its core; from its mass and stage by standard stellar models        |
| `stellar/engines.sun`             | ppChain                     | `borexino2018`, `borexino2020`   | verified | measured by its neutrinos: the proton–proton chain, with the CNO cycle supplying about 1 %                                                  |
| `stellar/engines.procyon`         | cnoCycle or hydrogenShell   | `kippenhahn2012`                 | derived  | from its mass and stage by standard stellar models: near the end of core hydrogen burning, perhaps already burning it in a shell            |
| `stellar/engines.sirius`          | cnoCycle                    | `kippenhahn2012`                 | derived  | from its mass and stage by standard stellar models: a main-sequence star above 1.3 M☉                                                       |
| `stellar/engines.vega`            | cnoCycle                    | `kippenhahn2012`                 | derived  | from its mass and stage by standard stellar models: a main-sequence star above 1.3 M☉                                                       |
| `stellar/engines.achernar`        | cnoCycle                    | `kippenhahn2012`                 | derived  | from its mass and stage by standard stellar models: a main-sequence B star                                                                  |
| `stellar/engines.arcturus`        | hydrogenShell or heliumCore | `kippenhahn2012`                 | derived  | from its mass and stage by standard stellar models: a red giant on the red-giant branch, or already burning helium in its core              |
| `stellar/engines.alnitak`         | cnoCycle or hydrogenShell   | `kippenhahn2012`                 | derived  | from its mass and stage by standard stellar models: an O supergiant late in core hydrogen burning, or just past it                          |
| `stellar/engines.r136a1`          | cnoCycle                    | `crowther2010`, `kippenhahn2012` | **flag** | a hydrogen-rich WN5h star, about a million years old, still burning hydrogen in its core                                                    |
| `stellar/engines.aldebaran`       | hydrogenShell or heliumCore | `kippenhahn2012`                 | derived  | from its mass and stage by standard stellar models: a red giant on the red-giant branch, or already burning helium in its core              |
| `stellar/engines.rigel`           | heliumCore or hydrogenShell | `kippenhahn2012`                 | derived  | from its mass and stage by standard stellar models: a blue supergiant past the main sequence; whether its core burns helium yet is debated  |
| `stellar/engines.deneb`           | heliumCore or hydrogenShell | `kippenhahn2012`                 | derived  | from its mass and stage by standard stellar models: a white supergiant past the main sequence; whether its core burns helium yet is debated |
| `stellar/engines.antares`         | heliumCore                  | `kippenhahn2012`                 | derived  | from its mass and stage by standard stellar models: a red supergiant                                                                        |
| `stellar/engines.betelgeuse`      | heliumCore                  | `joyce2020`                      | verified | early core helium burning, from its pulsations and evolutionary models                                                                      |
| `stellar/engines.uy-scuti`        | heliumCore                  | `kippenhahn2012`                 | derived  | from its mass and stage by standard stellar models: a red supergiant, in core helium burning or later                                       |
| `stellar/engines.vy-cma`          | heliumCore                  | `kippenhahn2012`                 | derived  | from its mass and stage by standard stellar models: a red hypergiant, in core helium burning or later                                       |
| `stellar/engines.stephenson-2-18` | heliumCore                  | `kippenhahn2012`                 | derived  | from its mass and stage by standard stellar models: a red supergiant, in core helium burning or later                                       |
| `stellar/engines.cygnus-x1`       | accretion                   | `millerJones2021`                | derived  | no fusion: gas pulled from its blue supergiant companion heats up as it falls in                                                            |
| `stellar/engines.gaia-bh3`        | dormant                     | `panuzzo2024`                    | derived  | no fusion and no feeding: its giant companion is too far off to spill gas, so it gives out no light                                         |
| `stellar/engines.gw231123`        | dormant                     | `lvk2025`                        | derived  | no fusion and no feeding: seen only by the gravitational waves of the merger that made it                                                   |
| `stellar/engines.omega-cen`       | dormant                     | `haberle2024`                    | derived  | no fusion and no feeding: found only by the stars it flings about the cluster's core                                                        |
| `stellar/engines.sgr-a`           | accretion                   | `gravity2022`                    | derived  | no fusion: a thin trickle of infalling gas, faint and flickering                                                                            |
| `stellar/engines.m87`             | accretion                   | `eht2019`                        | derived  | no fusion: its infalling gas glows and launches a jet                                                                                       |
| `stellar/engines.ton-618`         | accretion                   | `ge2019`                         | derived  | no fusion: a quasar, lit by gas falling in at a furious rate                                                                                |
| `giants/engines.earth`            | radioactivity               | `kamland2011`                    | verified | no fusion: radioactive decay supplies about half its heat, the rest is left from its formation                                              |
| `giants/engines.jupiter`          | contraction                 | `li2018`                         | derived  | no fusion: it radiates more heat than it absorbs, left from its formation and released as it slowly contracts and cools                     |

## Deep Field: our reach

`solar/probes.ts`: the five probes fly their JPL Horizons trajectories
(`probe-tracks.json`, fetched by `scripts/fetch-probe-tracks.mjs`: heliocentric, ecliptic
J2000, monthly through the flybys and twice a year after, to 2040), read at the visitor's date.
The Voyagers' boundary distances in the card catalogue are those tracks read on the published
crossing dates, and land on the published distances. The dates below were not reopened.

## Orrery: observatories near Earth

`solar/observatories.ts`: Hubble and Chandra move on their CelesTrak element sets
(`observatory-orbits.json`, fetched by `scripts/fetch-observatory-orbits.mjs`; the epochs are in
the file and the catalogue's notes). The mean motion gives the size by Kepler's third law about
Earth's GM; Earth's J2 (IERS 2010) turns the node and perigee at their secular rates, and the
element set's drag term (half the mean motion's rate of change) moves the place on as t². The
elements are SGP4 mean elements of the equator of date, read as two-body elements of the J2000
equator (the frames differ by under half a degree): good for the orbit's size, tilt and plane
for months; the place along the orbit drifts within days to weeks (changing drag, lunisolar
pulls). Webb flies its
JPL Horizons trajectory (Goddard FDF: reconstructed, then predicted to October 2031; every four
days, to 100 km), stored in the frame turning with Earth round the Sun and turned back with the
Earth the scene draws; past the end the scene holds its last place. The L2 point is the
restricted three-body solution for the Sun and the Earth–Moon pair at one au, from the shared
GMs; NASA gives 1.5 million km. Euclid is left out: no Euclid 3D model under a licence that
allows redistribution was found (the Euclid Consortium's printable model is for non-commercial
use only). Gaia, retired to a heliocentric orbit in March 2025, is left out too: no
sourced position.

## Voyage: the ship and its drives

`voyage/parameters.ts`, `voyage/anchors.ts`, `voyage/comoving.ts` and `voyage/barycentre.ts`:
what the ship can do and where it can go. Trips are closed-form special relativity
(`toolkit/relativity.ts`); the ship's own integrator steps the proper velocity by the
four-acceleration and is tested against hyperbolic motion.

## Bright-star distances and temperatures

`space/star-distances.json` holds a distance for all 188 catalogue stars, written by
`scripts/fetch-star-distances.mjs` from SIMBAD TAP (20 Oct 2026): each star is found by a 3′ cone
around its catalogue position (the brightest object there with a parallax, so multiple systems
resolve to their primary; for the four combined entries without a V flux, the nearest parallax in
the cone), and the distance is 1000 / `plx_value`. SIMBAD adopts Gaia DR3 where it has one and
Hipparcos (van Leeuwen 2007) otherwise; the file's `references` field counts each bibcode.
`starDistance` (`space/star-distances.ts`) prefers the shared catalogue's literature distance
for the stars `space/stellar.ts` describes (Rigil Kentaurus as α Cen A, Sirius, Vega, Achernar,
Arcturus, Alnitak, Aldebaran, Rigel, Deneb, Antares, Betelgeuse), so every simulation places
them alike; SIMBAD's parallax serves the rest.

| Where                       | Quantity               | Value                                                   | Source                                                                                                                                                            | Status          | Note                                                                                                  |
| --------------------------- | ---------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `space/star-distances.json` | 110 stars' parallaxes  | pc                                                      | van Leeuwen 2007, A&A 474, 653, [doi:10.1051/0004-6361:20078357](https://doi.org/10.1051/0004-6361:20078357) (Hipparcos, the new reduction), via SIMBAD           | verified        | the bright stars Gaia saturates on; Sirius 2.64, Vega 7.68, Polaris 133, Betelgeuse 153, Deneb 433 pc |
| `space/star-distances.json` | 73 stars' parallaxes   | pc                                                      | Gaia Collaboration 2020, Gaia EDR3 / DR3 (I/350) and Gaia DR2 (I/345), [doi:10.1051/0004-6361/202039657](https://doi.org/10.1051/0004-6361/202039657), via SIMBAD | verified        | fainter catalogue stars                                                                               |

## Additional rendering and surface references

These references support illustrative rendering choices and physical constraints;
they do not supply observed textures unless explicitly identified in the imagery
credits. The public source record also includes both technical bibliographies and
the live catalogue source registry.

- `toolkit/surfaces/giants.ts`: Simon et al. 2024, PSJ 5, 223 (Great Red Spot);
  Chavez et al. 2023, Icarus 404 (Neptune cloud variability); Irwin et al. 2024,
  MNRAS 527, 11521 (Uranus and Neptune colours).
- `toolkit/surfaces/planets.ts`: Titov et al. 2018, Space Sci. Rev. 214, 126
  (Venus clouds); Barucci et al. 2005, Emery et al. 2007, Pál et al. 2012 and
  Gaudi et al. 2005 (Sedna constraints); Keller et al. 1986/1987 and
  Stooke & Abergel 1991 (Halley). The generated maps are Horizon illustrations.
- `toolkit/surfaces/moons.ts`: Smith et al. 1981; Porco et al. 2005;
  Lorenz et al. 2004; Roman et al. 2009 (Titan atmosphere and haze).
- `toolkit/surfaces/index.ts`: Buratti et al. 2017 (New Horizons albedos),
  Pál et al. 2012 (Sedna), and the NASA planetary fact sheets.
- `orrery/ring.frag.glsl`: Chandrasekhar 1960, Radiative Transfer;
  Cuzzi et al. 1984 (single-scattering slab approximation).
- `toolkit/temperature.glsl`: Tanner Helland 2012, temperature-to-RGB fit,
  based on Mitchell Charity's blackbody colour table. The GLSL adaptation's
  BSD-2-Clause notice is included in the public third-party notices.
- `orrery/solar-activity*.ts`: Parenti 2014, LRSP 11, 1 (prominence plasma, hedgerow
  threads, barbs, flows); Gibson 2018, LRSP 15, 7 (the magnetic dips and cavity);
  Vial & Engvold 2015, ASSL 415 (sizes); Zirker, Engvold & Martin 1998, Nature 396, 440
  (counter-streaming); Arregui, Oliver & Ballester 2018, LRSP 15, 3 (large-amplitude
  oscillations); Sterling & Moore 2005, ApJ 630, 1148 and Chen 2011, LRSP 8, 1 (slow then
  fast rise of eruptions); NASA/MSFC solar features,
  <https://solarscience.msfc.nasa.gov/feature2.shtml>;
  <https://www.nasa.gov/image-article/what-solar-prominence/>;
  <https://www.nasa.gov/image-article/suns-corona-seen-during-2017-solar-eclipse/>.
  Heights, lengths, thread speeds and the dilution of photospheric light are physical;
  shapes, positions, the cycle and its timing are illustrative, not observations.
- `toolkit/science/laws.ts`: Kepler, Astronomia Nova (1609) and Harmonices
  Mundi (1619); Schwarzschild (1916); Sinnott 1984, Sky & Telescope 68, 159;
  Hubble 1929, PNAS 15, 168; Lemaître (1927).
- `bh/atlas.ts`: Maíz Apellániz et al. 2022, A&A 657, A131 (Orion distance
  context), in addition to the atlas-site references above.

Public attribution maintenance: run `npm run credits:generate` after changing this
ledger, either `technical.ts`, the catalogue `SOURCES` registry or shared
constant citations. `npm test` checks that the public record remains current.
This attribution audit does not remove the measurement flags below.

## Open flags

- Planetesimals by ~0.1 Myr and Moon-mass embryos by ~1 Myr (`orrery/parameters.ts`: `PLANETESIMALS_AFTER`, `EMBRYOS_AFTER`): consistent with reviews; no primary text was readable.
- Moon Cassini angle 1.5424° (`toolkit/moons.ts`): IAU amplitudes give 1.542–1.544°; the fourth decimal (Eckhardt) was not opened.
- Halley's 2.2-day spin (`toolkit/figures.ts`): Belton et al. 1991 model a 3.69 d precession with a 7.1 d long-axis spin; the drawn period is a labelled teaching choice.
- Saturn B-ring albedo 0.5 used to scale ring colours (`toolkit/rings.ts`): no primary opened.
- Stellar photometry flags above: values kept as the combined light a naked eye sees, or where SIMBAD lacks data.
- Perseus Cluster cz 5,366 km/s (`cosmos/cosmos.ts`, `WEB_SKY.perseus`): Struble & Rood 1999 seen only as quoted; NED gives NGC 1275 itself 5,297 km/s, which moves the distance by about 1 %.
- Deep Field's "On Earth" boundaries (`deep-field/depth.ts`: `EARTH_THEN`): standard figures, sources not reopened.
- The probes' launch and boundary-crossing dates (`solar/probes.ts`: `PROBES`): standard mission dates, sources not reopened; the crossing distances read from the Horizons tracks on those dates match the published 94, 84, 121.6 and 119 au.
- Narrative history without numbers (Kant and Laplace, aluminium-26 and a nearby massive star, Arrokoth) was not treated as a measurement.
